\title{Benign Overfitting in Single-Head Attention}

\begin{abstract}
The phenomenon of \emph{benign overfitting}, where a trained neural network perfectly fits noisy training data but still achieves near-optimal test performance, has been extensively studied in recent years for linear models and fully-connected/convolutional networks. In this work, we study benign overfitting in a single-head softmax attention model, which is the fundamental building block of Transformers. We prove that under appropriate conditions, the model exhibits benign overfitting in a classification setting already after two steps of gradient descent. Moreover, we show conditions where a minimum-norm/maximum-margin interpolator exhibits benign overfitting. We study how the overfitting behavior depends on the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) of the data distribution, namely, the ratio between norms of signal and noise tokens, and prove that a sufficiently large SNR is both necessary and sufficient for benign overfitting.

\end{abstract}

\section{Introduction}


Neural networks often exhibit a remarkable phenomenon, known as \emph{benign overfitting}, where they achieve a perfect fit to noisy training examples and still generalize well to unseen data \citep{zhang2021understanding, bartlett2020benign}. This phenomenon contradicts classical wisdom in machine learning, and has become a central research question in the theory of deep learning. 
Existing works on benign overfitting study under what conditions the phenomenon occurs in different architectures. These works focus on linear models, and on shallow fully-connected and convolutional neural networks. 

In recent years, Transformers \citep{vaswani2017attention} have emerged as a leading neural network architecture, with impactful applications across a wide range of domains such as natural language processing and computer vision. The fundamental building block of Transformers is the attention mechanism, which allows them to process sequences and focus different parts of the input. Despite the central role of the attention mechanism, we currently do not understand their overfitting behavior and the conditions under which they exhibit benign overfitting.

In this work, we show the first benign-overfitting results for the attention mechanism. We consider classification with a single-head softmax attention model, and study the conditions that allow for benign overfitting.  
In our results, the data distribution consists of two tokens: a \emph{signal token}, which can be used for correctly classifying clean test examples, and a \emph{noise token}, which is independent of the label but can be used for interpolating (i.e., perfectly fitting) noisy training examples. We study the singnal-to-noise ratio (SNR), namely, the expected ratio between the norms of signal and noise tokens, that allows for benign overfitting.

Below we summarize our main contributions: 
\begin{itemize}
    \item In Theorem~\ref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration} (\Cref{sec: gd}) we show that under appropriate conditions, gradient descent with the logistic loss exhibits benign overfitting already after two iterations. This result holds when the SNR is $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$, where $n$ is the number of training samples.

    \item We then turn to consider other natural learning rules, which allow for benign overfitting under a weaker requirement on the SNR. In Theorems~\ref{thrm: main_thrm_f} and~\ref{thm: bias-of-max-margin-joint-constrained-gamma} (\Cref{sec: benign-overfitting-of-optimal-solution}), we prove that minimum-norm (i.e., maximum-margin) interpolators exhibit benign overfitting when the SNR is $\Omega(1/\sqrt{n})$ without requiring an upper bound on the SNR.

    \item In Theorem~\ref{thrm: main_thrm_mm_s} (\Cref{sec: benign-overfitting-of-optimal-solution}), we prove that the above requirement on the SNR is tight. Namely, if the SNR is smaller than it, then the min-norm interpolator exhibits harmful overfitting, where it fits the training data but has poor generalization performance.

    \item In Section~\ref{sec:experiments}, we complement our theoretical results with an empirical study. We show that a sufficiently large SNR and input dimension are necessary to achieve benign overfitting.
\end{itemize}

The paper is structured as follows. In Section~\ref{sec:prelim}, we provide some preliminaries and define the data distribution and the single-head attention model. In Sections~\ref{sec: gd} and~\ref{sec: benign-overfitting-of-optimal-solution} we state our main results on benign overfitting with gradient descent and with min-norm interpolators. Finally, in Section~\ref{sec:experiments} we show empirical results.
\subsection*{Related Work}
\textbf{Optimization in Transformers.} \citet{li2023theoretical} provided a theoretical analysis of training a shallow Vision Transformer (ViT) for a classification task. They showed that the sample complexity required to achieve a zero generalization error is correlated with the inverse of the fraction of label-relevant tokens, the token noise level, and the initial model error. 
\citet{tarzanagh2023transformers} showed that optimizing the attention layer via gradient descent leads to convergence to an SVM solution, where the implicit bias of the attention mechanism depends on whether the parameters are represented as a product of key-query matrices or directly as a combined matrix, with different norm-minimization objectives in each case.
\citet{ataee2023max} provided a regularization path analysis and prove that the attention weights converge in a direction to a max-margin solution that separates locally optimal tokens from non-optimal. They also showed that running gradient descent, with a specific  initialization direction and without optimizing the attention head, converges in a direction to the same max-margin solution.
\cite{vasudeva2024implicit} expanded on their findings by identifying non-trivial data settings for which the convergence of GD is provably global, i.e., without requiring assumptions about the initialization direction. They also provided convergence rate bounds and analysis for optimizing both the attention weights and the attention head, although they did not consider the case of noisy data labels, as we do in our work.  Another line of work looks at the learning dynamics of single-layer linear attention models trained on linear regression tasks~\citep{zhang2023trained,ahn2023transformers,wu2023pretraining}. 
Additional works that consider optimization dynamics in Transformers include~\cite{jelassi2022vision, oymak2023role}.
\textbf{Benign overfitting.} 
A significant body of research has explored why neural networks (NNs) that perfectly interpolate the training data can still generalize well \citep{zhang2021understanding,bartlett2020benign}. This has sparked substantial interest in studying overfitting and generalization in NNs trained to fit datasets with noisy labels.
The literature on benign overfitting is broad and cannot be reasonably covered here. We refer the reader to the surveys \citet{bartlett2021statisticalviewpoint,belkin2021survey}. 
Most relevant to our work are \citet{cao22conv,kou2023benign,meng2023benign} that studied benign overfitting in convolutional neural networks. Their data distribution resembles ours, as we discuss in Section~\ref{sec:data.generation}. Benign overffiting in fully-connected two-layer neural network classification was studied in \citet{frei2022benign,frei2023benign, xu2023benign, xu2023bbenign,  kornowski2024tempered,george2024training, karhadkar2024benign} for various activation functions, data distributions and loss functions (both the logistic and the hinge losses).




\section{Preliminaries}

\textbf{Notations.} We use bold-face letters to denote vectors and matrices, and let $[m]$ be shorthand for $\{1,2,\dots,m\}$. Given a vector $\bx$, we denote by $x_j$ its $j$-th coordinate.  Let $\bI_{d}$ be the $d\times d$ identity matrix, and let $\zero_d$ (or just $\zero$, if $d$ is clear from the context) denote the zero vector in $\mathbb{R}^d$. We let $\norm{\cdot}$ denote the Euclidean norm. We denote a multivariate Gaussian
distribution with mean vector $\bmu$ and covariance matrix $\mSigma$ by $N(\bmu, \mSigma)$. 
We use standard big-Oh notation, with  $\Theta(\cdot), \Omega(\cdot),O(\cdot)$ hiding universal constants and $\Tilde{\Theta}(\cdot), \Tilde{\Omega}(\cdot),\Tilde{O}(\cdot)$ hiding constants and factors that are polylogarithmic in the problem parameters. We use $\mathbb{I}(\cdot)$ to denote the indicator variable of an event. For a finite set $\mathcal{A}$, denote the uniform distribution over $\mathcal{A}$ by $\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{A})$ and let $|\mathcal{A}|$ be its cardinality.
\subsection{Data Generation Setting} \label{sec:data.generation}

In this work we focus on the following data distribution:

\begin{definition}
    Let $\bmu_1, \bmu_2 \in \mathbb{R}^d$ such that $\norm{\bmu_1} = \norm{\bmu_2} = \rho$ 
    for some $\rho > 0$
    and $\la \bmu_1,\bmu_2 \ra = 0$, be two fixed orthogonal vectors representing the signal contained in each data point. Define $\mathcal{D}_{\text{clean}}$ as the distribution over $\mathbb{R}^{2 \times d} \times \{\pm 1\}$ of labelled data such that a data point $(\bX, \tilde y)$ is generated by the following procedure:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Sample the label $\tilde y \sim \mathsf{Unif}\{\pm 1\}$.
        \item Generate 
        a vector $\ub$, which represents the signal,
        as follows:
        If $\tilde y = +1$, set $\ub = \bmu_1$; and if $\tilde y = -1$, set $\ub = \bmu_2$.
        \item Generate a vector $\bxi$, which represents the noise, from the Gaussian distribution $\bxi \sim \mathcal{N}(\zero, \bI_{d} - \bmu_1\bmu_1^{\top}/\rho^2 -  \bmu_2\bmu_2^{\top}/\rho^2)$. 
        \item Denote $\bX = (\bx^{(1)}, \bx^{(2)})^{\top}$. Select $k \sim \mathsf{Unif}\{1,2\}$ and set $\bx^{(k)} = \ub$. Set the other token $\bx^{(3-k)} = \bxi$. 
    \end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

To study the overfitting behavior we also need to introduce label-flipping noise:

\begin{definition}
    Let $\eta \in [0, 1/2)$ be the label flipping probability.
    We define $\mathcal{D}$ as the distribution over $\mathbb{R}^{2 \times d} \times \{\pm 1\}$ which is the $\eta$-label-flipped version of $\mathcal{D}_{\text{clean}}$. Namely, to generate $(\bX, y) \sim \mathcal{D}$, first generate $(\bX, \tilde y) \sim \mathcal{D}_{\text{clean}}$, then let $y = \tilde y$ with probability $1-\eta$ and $y = - \tilde y$ with probability $\eta$.
\end{definition}

Our data 
distribution resembles the distributions considered by \citet{kou2023benign,cao22conv,meng2023benign}. They proved benign overfitting in two-layer convolutional neural networks, and in their setting each data point consists of two patches $\bx^{(1)},\bx^{(2)}$ (rather than two tokens in our setting).
Since our single-head attention model is invariant to the order of the tokens, we assume without loss of generality throughout this work that $\bx^{(1)}$ is the signal token and $\bx^{(2)}$ is the noise token in all data points.
Note that the noise token $\bx^{(2)}=\bxi$ is independent of the label, and that it is generated from $\mathcal{N}(\zero, \bI_{d} - \bmu_1\bmu_1^{\top}/\rho^2 -  \bmu_2\bmu_2^{\top}/\rho^2)$, ensuring that it is orthogonal to the signal vector. Note that when the dimension $d$ is large, $\|\bxi\| \approx \sqrt{d-2} \approx \sqrt{d}$ by standard concentration bounds. Therefore, we denote the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) as $\mathrm{SNR} = \|\bmu\|/\sqrt{d} = \rho/\sqrt{d}$.


We consider a training dataset $\{(\bX_i, y_i)\}^n_{i=1}$ of $n$ samples generated i.i.d. from the distribution $\mathcal{D}$.
Denote the index set of data whose labels are not flipped by  \(\itc = \{i: \tilde y_i = y_i \}\) (``clean examples''), and the index set of data whose labels are flipped by \(\itn = \{i: \tilde y_i = - y_i\}\) (``noisy examples''). For indices in \(\itc\), we further denote 
$
\itco := \itc \cap \{i: \bx_i^{(1)} = \bmu_1\},  %\quad 
\itct := \itc \cap \{i: \bx_i^{(1)} = \bmu_2\},
$
and define the subsets $\itno$, $\itnt$ of $\itn$ analogously. 
\subsection{Single-Head Attention Model}

We consider the following single-head attention model:
\begin{equation*} \label{eq:model1}
    f(\bX; \bW,\pb) = \vb^\top \bX^\top \SM(\bX \bW \qb)~,
\end{equation*}
where $\SM : \R^d \rightarrow \R^d$ is the softmax function, the key-query matrix $\bW \in \R^{d \times d}$ and the linear head vector $\vb \in \R^d$ are the trainable parameters, and the query vector $\qb \in \R^d$ is an arbitrary fixed unit vector. 
We follow \citet{ataee2023max} and assume
that $\qb = (1,0,\ldots,0)^\top$, obtaining the following model:
\begin{align}\label{eq:model}
    f(\bX; \pb, \vb) = \vb^{\top} \bX^{\top} \SM(\bX \pb),
\end{align}
Here the trained parameters are $\pb, \vb \in \R^d$. Thus, instead of the key-query matrix $\bW$ we have a vector $\pb$ that controls the attention. 
Throughout this paper we will use the model~(\ref{eq:model}).
We denote the output of the softmax layer \(\mathbb{S}(\bX_i \pb)\) by $\bs_i = (s_{i,1}, s_{i,2})^{\top}$, and 
denote the output of the attention layer \(\Xb_i^{\top}\bs_i\) by $\rb_i = s_{i,1} \bmu_i + s_{i,2} \bxi_i$, where $0 \le s_{i,1}, s_{i,2} \le 1$, $s_{i,1}+s_{i,2}=1$ are the attention on two tokens of the $i$-th sample.  





\section{Benign Overfitting with Gradient Descent}


In this section, we study the joint optimization of the head $\bv$ and attention weights $\bp$ using the logistic loss function. We show that the model exhibits benign overfitting after just two iterations of gradient descent (GD).

Formally, for a training dataset $\{(\bX_i, y_i)\}^n_{i=1}$ we define the empirical risk as
\begin{align*}
    \mathcal{L}(\vb, \pb) = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \ell(y_i \cdot f(\bX_i; \pb, \vb)),
\end{align*}
where $\ell(z) = \log(1+\exp(-z))$ is the logistic loss function, and $f$ is the model from Eq.~(\ref{eq:model}). We consider GD optimization. Starting from $\pb_0 = \zero$ and $\vb_0 = \zero$, we have
\begin{align*}
    \vb_{t+1} = \vb_t - \steps \nabla_{\vb}  \mathcal{L}(\vb_{t}, \pb_{t}) 
    \quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad
    \pb_{t+1} = \pb_t - \steps \nabla_{\pb}  \mathcal{L}(\vb_{t}, \pb_{t}),
\end{align*}
where $\steps$ is the step size. 
When we discuss some fixed $t$, we sometimes write in the subscript ``$t=\cdot$'', e.g., $\pb_{t=2}$ instead of $\pb_2$.
We make the following assumptions:
\begin{assumption} [Assumptions for GD with SNR = $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$] \label{assumption: gd_main}
Let $\delta \in (0, 0.5)$ be a desired probability of failure.
For universal constants $C_{\rho} \geq 6, C_{\beta} \geq 16$, as well as a sufficiently large universal constant $C$ that may depend on $C_{\rho}$ and $C_{\beta}$, the following conditions hold:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Number of samples $n$ is sufficiently large: $n \geq C\log(1/\delta)$.\label{assumption: gd-number-of-samples-is-big_main}
        \item Dimension $d$ is sufficiently large: $d \ge C n^2 \log(n/\delta)$.\label{assumption: gd-high-dimmension_main}
        \item Signal strength satisfies $\rho = C_{\rho} \cdot \sqrt{d/n}$ \label{assumption: gd-signal-strength_main}
        \item Label flipping rate satisfies $\eta \leq 1/C$. \label{assumption: gd-label-flipping-rate_main}
        \item Step size satisfies $\beta = C_{\beta} \cdot (n/d)$. \label{assumption: gd-step-size_main}
        \item Initialization at zero: $\norm{\vb_0} = \norm{\pb_0} = 0$. \label{assumption: gd-initialization-at-zero_main}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}
Item \ref{assumption: gd-number-of-samples-is-big_main} 
is required to estimate the number of clean examples compared to noisy examples. The assumption of high dimensionality (Item \ref{assumption: gd-high-dimmension_main}) is important
for enabling benign overfitting (see empirical results in Section~\ref{sec:experiments}), and implies that noise tokens from different training samples are nearly-orthogonal.
This assumption appears in many prior works on benign overfitting in neural network classification (e.g., \citet{cao22conv,kou2023benign,meng2023benign,frei2022benign,frei2023benign,xu2023benign,kornowski2024tempered,xu2023bbenign}).
Item \ref{assumption: gd-signal-strength_main} states that the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) is $\tfrac{\rho}{\sqrt{d}} = \Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$. In Section~\ref{sec:ideas} we will discuss how the SNR affects the dynamics of GD.
Interestingly, SNR of $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$ matches the lower bound of the required SNR that allows for benign overfitting with the min-norm (i.e. max-margin) learning rule that we will study in Section~\ref{sec: benign-overfitting-of-optimal-solution}.
Item \ref{assumption: gd-label-flipping-rate_main} ensures the flipping rate is small enough to allow the model to learn the signal token. 
Item \ref{assumption: gd-step-size_main}, namely, using a step size of $\Theta(n/d)$, is required to achieve benign overfitting after two iterations; with a smaller step size, the model will need more iterations to fit the noisy samples, which we will demonstrate empirically in Section~\ref{sec:experiments}.   
We now state our main result on benign overfitting with GD:

\begin{theorem} \label{thm: gd-after-2-iteration}
    Suppose that Assumption \ref{assumption: gd_main} holds. Then, with probability at least $1-\delta$ over the training dataset, after two iterations of GD we have:
   \begin{itemize}
    \item Higher softmax probability for optimal tokens:
    \begin{align*}
        s_{i,1}^{t=2} > 1/2, \; \forall i \in \clean 
        \quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad
         s_{i,2}^{t=2} \geq 1-1/\signalconst^2, \; \forall i \in \noise 
    \end{align*}
    where 
    $s_{i,j}^t$ is the softmax probability of the $j^{\text{th}}$ token in the $i^{\text{th}}$ sample at time $t$.
    \item The classifier $\bX \mapsto \sign(f(\bX; \vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2}))$ correctly classifies all training data points:
    $$
    y_i = \sign(f(\bX_i; \vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2})),\  \forall i \in [n].
    $$
    \item The classifier $\bX \mapsto \sign(f(\bX;\vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2})$ generalizes well:
    $$
      \mathbb{P}_{(\bX,y) \sim \mathcal{D}}(y \neq \sign(f(\bX; \vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2}))) \leq
     \eta + \exp(-d/C_1 n^2) ,
    $$ 
    where $C_1 := C_1(\signalconst,\stepSizeConst)$ is a constant.
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}
    We can also conclude that for the clean-labeled distribution $\mathcal{D}_{\text{clean}}$ we have  $$\mathbb{P}_{(\bX,y) \sim \mathcal{D}_{\text{clean}}}(y \neq \sign(f(\bX; \vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2}))) \leq \exp(-d/C_1 n^2),$$  which approaches zero as $d$ grows (see Assumption \ref{assumption: gd_main}, item \ref{assumption: gd-high-dimmension_main}).

\Cref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration} shows that after two iterations of GD, the attention focuses on the signal tokens for clean examples, and on the noise tokens for noisy examples. The model uses the noise tokens for interpolating noisy training examples, while still achieving good generalization performance using the signal token.


\section{Benign Overfitting of Max-Margin Solution}



In the previous section we showed that GD exhibits benign overfitting in a setting where the SNR is $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$.
We now turn to study the overfitting behavior of single-head attention models, when using another learning rule, which returns solutions that interpolate the training data with large margin while keeping the parameters norms small. As we will show, such a learning rule allows us to obtain benign overfitting under a weaker requirement on the SNR, namely, the SNR is $\Omega(1/\sqrt{n})$ without requiring an upper bound on it. 

We note that learning rules that return min-norm (or max-margin) solutions are considered natural, and hence understanding properties of min-norm interpolators has attracted much interest in recent years, even in settings where the implicit bias of GD does not necessarily lead to a min-norm solution (see, e.g., \citet{savarese2019infinite, ongie2019function, ergen2021convex, hanin2021ridgeless, debarre2022sparsest, boursier2023penalising}). More directly related to our work, min-norm interpolation with Transformers has been studied in \citet{ataee2023max,tarzanagh2023transformers}, and
benign/tempered overfitting in min-norm univariate neural network interpolators has been studied in \citet{joshi2023noisy}.

We first consider the following learning rule:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: problem_def}
    (\vb_{(r,R)}, \pb_{(r,R)}) = \argmax\limits_{\|\vb\| \le r, \|\pb\| \le R} \min_{i \in [n]} y_i \cdot f(\bX_i; \pb, \vb)~,
\end{equation}
where $f$ is the model from \eqref{eq:model}. The learning rule returns a solution that maximizes the margin $\min_{i \in [n]} y_i \cdot f(\bX_i; \pb, \vb)$ under a restriction on the parameter norms.
We make the following assumption:
\begin{assumption}[Assumptions for max-margin with SNR $= \Omega(1/\sqrt{n})$]
\label{cond: main_cond_f}
Let $\delta \in (0, 0.5)$ be a desired probability of failure. There exists a sufficiently large constant $C$ such that the following hold:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Dimension $d$ is sufficiently large: $d \ge C n^2 \log(n/\delta)$. \label{assumptionmm: dimension_large}
        \item Number of samples $n$ is sufficiently large: $n \geq C\log(1/\delta)$. \label{assumptionmm: samplesize_large}
        \item Signal strength: $\rho \ge C \sqrt{d/n}$. \label{assumptionmm: SNR_large}
        \item Label flipping rate: $0 \le \eta \le 1/C$. \label{assumptionmm: label_flipping_small}
        \item Norm constraint of $\pb$ satisfies: $R \ge C \sqrt{\eta n / d + 1/\rho^2}\log(\rho n)$. \label{assumptionmm: R_large}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}

Items \ref{assumptionmm: dimension_large}, \ref{assumptionmm: samplesize_large} and \ref{assumptionmm: label_flipping_small} are similar to Assumption~\ref{assumption: gd_main}.
Item \ref{assumptionmm: SNR_large} 
requires $\mathrm{SNR} \geq \Omega(1/\sqrt{n})$, which is a weaker requirement than the $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$ requirement in Assumption~\ref{assumption: gd_main}. We will show later a lower bound on the required SNR for benign overfitting, implying that the $\Omega(1/\sqrt{n})$ bound is tight.
Item \ref{assumptionmm: R_large} provides the lower bound for the norm constraint of $\pb$ so that the model can allocate enough attention on signal token to achieve benign overfitting. Note that the norm constraint $r$ for $\vb$ can take any positive value. Intuitively, since the model is linear in $\vb$, once $\pb$ is properly learned, $\vb$ can achieve accurate classification even with a small norm. %, highlighting the advantage of the attention mechanism over other models.

With these assumptions in place, we give our result on benign overfitting with the learning rule~(\ref{eq: problem_def}).
\begin{theorem}
\label{thrm: main_thrm_f}
Suppose that Assumption \ref{cond: main_cond_f} holds, 
and consider the classifier $\bX \rightarrow \sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)}))$, where $(\vb_{(r,R)},\pb_{(r, R)})$ is the solution to Problem~(\ref{eq: problem_def}). 
Then,
with probability at least $1 - \delta$ over the training dataset, we have:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The classifier $\sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)}))$ correctly classifies all training data points:
    $$
    y_i = \sign(f(\bX_i; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)})),\  \forall i \in [n].
    $$
    \item The classifier $\sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)}))$ generalizes well on test data:
    \begin{align*}
    \mathbb{P}_{(\bX, y) \sim \mathcal{D}}&(y \neq \sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)}))) 
    \\
    &\le \eta + \exp(-\Omega(d/ n^2))+\exp\Big(-\Omega\big(\frac{(1 - \zeta)}{\sqrt{\eta n /d + 1/\rho^2}} - \frac{\log(d)}{R}\big)^2 \Big),
    \end{align*} 
    where %$C_1 = \Theta(1)$ and 
    $\zeta = \Theta(\sqrt{\eta n/d + 1/\rho^2} \log(\rho n)/R)$.
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark} \label{remark: asymptotic_max_margin}
    To see why Theorem~\ref{thrm: main_thrm_f} implies benign overfitting, consider the limit $R \rightarrow \infty$. Then,
    the upper bound for test error 
    becomes \(\eta + \exp(-\Omega(d/ n^2)) + \exp(-\Theta((1/\rho^2 + \eta n/d)^{-1}))\), %is the same as Theorem \ref{thrm: main_thrm_if}.
    which can be arbitrarily close to $\eta$ if $d$ is large (see Assumption~\ref{cond: main_cond_f}, item~\ref{assumptionmm: dimension_large}).
\end{remark}
Next, we consider the following learning rule, which explicitly requires to minimize the parameters norms while allowing interpolation with margin at least $\gamma$:
\begin{align} 
    \left(\vb_{\gamma},\pb_{\gamma}\right) = \argmin_{\norm{\pb}^2 + \norm{\vb}^2}  \text{   s.t.   } \min_{i \in [n]} y_if(\bX_i; \pb,\vb) \geq \gamma~,  \label{problem: optimization_problem_joint_convergence_minimize_norm}
\end{align}
where $f$ is the model from Eq.~(\ref{eq:model}).
We show that under Assumption~\ref{cond: main_cond_f}, the solution $\left(\vb_{\gamma},\pb_{\gamma}\right)$ exhibits benign overfitting for large enough $\gamma$ and $d$: 
\begin{theorem} \label{thm: bias-of-max-margin-joint-constrained-gamma}
    Suppose that Assumption~\ref{cond: main_cond_f} (items~\ref{assumptionmm: dimension_large} through~\ref{assumptionmm: label_flipping_small})  holds, 
    and consider the classifier $\bX \rightarrow \sign(f(\bX; \pb_\gamma, \vb_\gamma))$, where $(\vb_\gamma,\pb_\gamma)$ is a solution of Problem~(\ref{problem: optimization_problem_joint_convergence_minimize_norm}).
    Then there exists $\gamma_0$ such that for any $\gamma \geq \gamma_0$ , with probability at least $1 - \delta$ over the training dataset, we have:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The classifier $\sign(f(\bX; \pb_{\gamma}, \vb_\gamma))$ correctly classifies all training data points:
    $$
    y_i = \sign(f(\bX_i; \pb_\gamma, \vb_\gamma)),\  \forall i \in [n].
    $$
    \item The classifier $\sign(f(\bX; \pb_\gamma, \vb_\gamma))$ generalizes well on test data:
    $$
    \mathbb{P}_{(\bX, y) \sim \mathcal{D}}(y \neq \sign(f(\bX; \pb_\gamma, \vb_\gamma))) \le  \eta + \exp(-\Omega(d/n^2)) + \exp(-\Theta((1/\rho^2 + \eta n/d)^{-1})).
    $$
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}

Thus, for large enough $\gamma$, the theorem implies that the trained model interpolates the training data, and the test error approaches $\eta$ as $d \to \infty$.

Note that Theorems~\ref{thrm: main_thrm_f} and~\ref{thm: bias-of-max-margin-joint-constrained-gamma} hold only when $\mathrm{SNR} =  \Omega(1/\sqrt{n})$. This 
raises the question: what is the overfitting behavior of min-norm interpolators when the SNR is smaller? We now
consider the case where $\rho \le \sqrt{1/C n}$ for some sufficiently large universal constant $C$. We will show that in this case, although the model can correctly classify all training samples, the test error of learning rule~(\ref{eq: problem_def}) is at least a universal constant, indicating that benign overfitting does not happen. 
Formally, we make the following assumptions:

\begin{assumption}[Assumptions for max-margin with SNR $\le O(1/\sqrt{n})$]

 Let $\delta \in (0. 0.5)$ be a desired probability of failure. There exists a sufficiently large constant $C$ such that the following hold:
\label{cond: main_cond_s}
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Dimension $d$ is sufficiently large: $d \ge C n^2 \log(n/\delta)$ \label{assumptionmm2: dimension_large}
        \item Number of samples $n$ is sufficiently large: $n \geq C\log(1/\delta)$. \label{assumptionmm2: samplesize_large}
        \item Signal strength: $\rho \le \sqrt{d/C n}$. \label{assumptionmm2: SNR_small}
        \item Label flipping rate is a constant $\eta \in (0,1/2)$. \label{assumptionmm2: labe_flipping_arb}
        \item The norm of $\pb$ should be sufficiently large: $R \ge C \sqrt{\frac{n}{d}} \log\big(\frac{n \rho}{d}\big)$. \label{assumptionmm2: R_large} 
    \end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}

Compared with Assumption \ref{cond: main_cond_f}, the main difference is in the second item that $\mathrm{SNR} \le O(1/\sqrt{n})$. Additionally, the condition on $\eta$ is relaxed, as in our analysis clean and noisy samples can be treated equivalently when the norm of the signal token is sufficiently small. 
With these assumptions in place, we can state the following theorem which characterizes the training error and test error of the single-head attention model when the SNR is small:
\begin{theorem}
\label{thrm: main_thrm_mm_s}
Suppose that Assumption \ref{cond: main_cond_s} holds, 
and consider the classifier $\bX \rightarrow \sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)}))$, where $(\vb_{(r,R)},\pb_{(r, R)})$ is a solution of Problem~(\ref{eq: problem_def}). 
Then, with probability at least $1 - \delta$ over the training data, we have:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The classifier $\sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)}))$ correctly classifies all training data points:
    $$
    y_i = \sign(f(\bX_i; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)})),\  \forall i \in [n].
    $$
    \item The classifier $\sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)}))$ does not generalize well on test data:
    $$
    \mathbb{P}_{(\bX, y) \sim \mathcal{D}_{\text{clean}}}(y \neq \sign(f(\bX; \pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r,R)})))  \ge \frac{1}{16}.
    $$
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}


\section{Proof ideas}


In this section we briefly discuss the main proof ideas.

\subsection{Proof ideas for Section~\ref{sec: gd}}

In this subsection we discuss the main proof idea of Theorem~\ref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration}.
Since the initialization is at zero, $\bv_t$ is a linear combination of the training data tokens. Therefore, we can express $\bv_{t=1}$ as $\lambda_1^{t=1} \bmu_1 + \lambda_2^{t=1} \bmu_2 + \sumn y_i\theta_i^{t=1}\bxi_i$, where $\lambda_1^{t=1} > 0, \lambda_2^{t=1} < 0$. Note that $\lambda_1^{t} > 0, \lambda_2^{t} < 0$ holds since $|\clean| > |\noise|$. We begin by analyzing the first step of GD. Specifically, we show that after one step, the coefficients of  $\bv_{t=1}$ can be estimated as $|\lambda_k^{t=1}| \approx \frac{\steps }{8}(1 - 2\eta), k\in [2]$ and $\theta_i^{t=1} = \frac{\beta}{4n}, i\in[n]$. Moreover, we have $\bp_{t=1} = 0$, and hence for a training sample $(\bX_j = (\bmu_k,\bxi_j), y_j)$, the margin is:
\begin{align*}
    y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) &= \frac{1}{2}y_j\vb_{t=1}^{\top}(\bx_{j}^{(1)}+\bx_{j}^{(2)}) \approx \frac{1}{2}y_j\lambda_k^{t=1}\norm{\bmu_k}^2 +  \frac{1}{2}\theta_j^{t=1}\norm{\bxi_j}^2,
\end{align*}
where in the last approximate equality we use the high dimensional setting (i.e. by item~\ref{assumption: gd-high-dimmension_main} in our assumption $d \gg n^2\log(n)$) to neglect the $\sum_{i\in[n]: i\neq j} y_iy_j \theta_j^{t=1}\bxi_i^{\top}\bxi_j$ term, since it is much smaller (in absolute value) than the other terms. 
Indeed, we have w.h.p. that 
$|\bxi_i^{\top}\bxi_j| %\approx
\leq
\sqrt{d}\log(n)$, $\norm{\bxi_j}^2 \approx d$ and recall that $\norm{\bmu_k}^2 = C_{\rho}^2(d/n)$ (item~\ref{assumption: gd-signal-strength_main} in our assumption).  Therefore, for a clean sample $j \in \clean$, the margin is $y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) \approx \frac{\steps(1-2\eta)}{16}\frac{d C_{\rho}^2}{n} +  \frac{\steps}{8n}d > 0,$
for large enough $ C_{\rho}$. On the other hand, for a noisy sample $j \in \noise$, we have  $y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) \approx -\frac{\steps(1-2\eta)}{16}\frac{d C_{\rho}^2}{n} +  \frac{\steps}{8n}d < 0$.
This implies that the classifier $\sign(f(\bX;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1})$ does not correctly classify noisy training samples, but still correctly classifies clean training samples. Together with $\bp_{t=1} = \zero$, the classifier $\sign(f(\bX;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1})$ will also correctly classify, with high probability, a clean test sample. 

Moreover, since the loss function $\ell$ is decreasing, the loss of noisy samples, denoted $\ell_{t=1,j}, j \in \noise$, dominates the loss of clean samples $\ell_{t=1,i}, i \in \clean$. This implies that after two iterations, the coefficients $|\theta_j^{t=2}|, j \in \noise$, of the second (noise) tokens in $\vb_{t=2}$, corresponding to noisy samples, grow faster than the coefficients $|\lambda_i^{t=2}|$ of the first (signal) tokens. 
This property is important to allow for interpolation of noisy examples.
We also show that
$\pb_{t=2}$ 
focuses on optimal tokens, 
namely, on the 
noise token
for noisy samples (i.e. $s_{i,2}^{t=2} \geq 1-1/\signalconst^2, \forall i \in \noise$), and on the signal token for 
clean training and test samples.
Using this property we conclude that the model parameterized by $(\vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2})$ exhibits benign overfitting.

\begin{remark} \label{rem:GD.behavior}
Note that our proof implies the following behavior of GD.
After the first iteration, %with high probability, 
the model correctly classifies only the clean training samples, resulting in an expected training accuracy of $1-\eta$. Additionally, the model successfully classifies a clean test sample w.h.p., leading to the same expected test accuracy. After the second iteration, the model interpolates the training data, achieving 
a
training accuracy of $1$. This is shown empirically in Figure \ref{fig:accuracies_large_step_size}. 
When using a smaller step size, we empirically observe a similar trend: after the first iteration, the model learns the signal tokens, and with more iterations, it captures the noisy tokens of the noisy samples and fits the entire dataset. This behavior  
is shown
in Figure \ref{fig:accuracies_small_step_size}. 
\end{remark}

\subsection{Proof ideas for Section~\ref{sec: benign-overfitting-of-optimal-solution}}

Here we provide the proof sketch for Theorem \ref{thrm: main_thrm_f}. There are mainly two parts in our proof:
\begin{itemize}
    \item First we determine the convergence behavior of $\pb$ and $\vb$ when the norm constraint $R$ is sufficiently large.
    
    \item Using properties derived from this convergence, we can analyze the training and test errors. 
\end{itemize}

The first part of the proof builds upon techniques from \citet{ataee2023max}, which shows that jointly solving for $\vb$ and $\pb$ leads to convergence to their respective max-margin solutions. While their approach focuses on the asymptotic case where $R, r \rightarrow \infty$ under specific conditions on the training data, our work extends these techniques to the signal-noise data model and provides non-asymptotic results.
To begin, consider the output of the attention layer \(\rb_i = \bX_i^{\top} \mathbb{S}(\bX_i \pb)\) which is a combination of signal and noise tokens. This can be considered as a ``token selection'' based on softmax probabilities. Since $\{\rb_i\}_{i \in [n]}$ determines the model's output, we prove that only by selecting signal tokens for clean samples and noise tokens for %nonclean 
noisy
samples can we reach the maximum margin when performing SVM on $(\rb_i,y_i)_{i \in [n]}$ and we refer to this as \emph{optimal tokens}.

\begin{definition}[Optimal Token]
    We define the \emph{optimal token} for sample $(\bX_i, y_i)$ as
    \begin{align}
    \rb_i^{\star} &= \bx_i^{(1)} = \bmu_k,\  i \in \itc_k, k \in \{1, 2\}
    \quad\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\quad
    \rb_i^{\star} = \bx_i^{(2)} = \bxi_i,\  i \in \itn \label{eq: best_token1_idea}
\end{align}
\end{definition}

Based on this optimal token selection, we define the corresponding max-margin solution for \(\pb\) and \(\vb\), denoted by $\pb_{mm}$ and $\vb_{mm}$. We first  define $\pb_{mm}$ as follows:

\begin{definition}[p-SVM]
\label{def: p-SVM_idea}
\begin{align}
    &\pb_{mm} = \argmin\limits_{\pb \in \mathbb{R}^d} \|\pb\| \;\;\;\;
    \text{subject to:} \nonumber
    \\
    \pb^{\top}(\bmu_k - &\bxi_i) \ge 1, i \in \itc_k\nonumber \;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;
    \pb^{\top}(\bxi_i - \bmu_i) \ge 1, i \in \itn \label{eq: p_SVM_cond_idea}
\end{align}
for all $k \in \{1, 2\}, i \in [n]$. 
Let $\Xi := 1/\|\pb_{mm}\|$ be the margin induced by \(\pb_{mm}\).
\end{definition}

 Then for a given \(\pb\), we define $\vb(\pb)$ as the standard max-margin classifier on $(\rb_i,y_i)_{i \in [n]}$
 and $\vb_{mm}$ as the standard max-margin classifier on $(\rb^{\star}_i,y_i)_{i \in [n]}$ which represents the limiting case when \(\pb = \pb_{mm}\) and \(R \rightarrow +\infty\).
\begin{definition}[v-SVM]
\label{def: labelmargin_idea}
\begin{equation}
    \vb(\pb) := \argmin_{\vb \in \mathbb{R}^d} \|\vb\|  \text{ s.t. } y_i\cdot \vb^{\top}\rb_i \geq 1, \quad \text{ for all } i \in [n]. \label{eq: SVM_cond_idea}
\end{equation}
\(\Gamma(\pb) := 1/\|\vb(\pb)\|\)
is the \textbf{label margin} induced by %$\vb$ and \(\pb\). 
$\vb(\pb)$.
When \(\rb_i = \rb_i^{\star}, i \in [n]\), we define
\begin{equation}
    \vb_{mm} := \argmin_{\vb \in \mathbb{R}^d} \|\vb\|  \text{ s.t. } y_i\cdot \vb^{\top}\rb_i^{\star} \geq 1, \quad \text{ for all } i \in [n]. \label{def: vmm_idea}
\end{equation}
\(\Gamma := 1/\|\vb_{mm}\|\)
is the label margin induced by $\vb_{mm}$.
\end{definition}
To show the optimality of this token selection, we prove that any other token selection that incorporates other tokens in \(\rb_i\) will strictly reduce the label margin. This is formalized in the following proposition:

\begin{restatable}[optimal token condition]{proposition}{Propoptimaltoken}\label{prop: optimal_token}
Suppose that Assumption \ref{cond: main_cond_f} holds, with probability at least $1-\delta$ over the training dataset, for all $\pb$,
the token selection under $\pb$ results in a label margin (Def. \ref{def: labelmargin_idea}) of at most $\Gamma - \frac{C}{\|\vb_{mm}\|^3 n \rho^2} \cdot \max\limits_{i \in [n]} (1 - s_{i \alpha_i})$ 
where $\alpha_i = \mathbb{I}(i \in \itc) + 2 \mathbb{I}(i \in \itn)$ and $C > 0$ is some constant.
\end{restatable}
Then, it is natural to make a conjecture that when jointly optimizing $\pb$ and $\vb$ for \eqref{eq: problem_def}, they will converge to their respective max-margin solutions $\pb_{mm}$ and $\vb_{mm}$ as $R, r \rightarrow \infty$. We verify and formalize it in the following theorem. 

\begin{restatable}{theorem}{thrmfiniteconverge}\label{thrm: finite_converge} 
    Suppose that Assumption \ref{cond: main_cond_f} holds, with probability at least $1-\delta$ on the training dataset, we have 
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The margin induced by $\pb_{(r, R)}/R$ in \textit{p-SVM} is at least $(1-\zeta)\Xi$, where
        \[
        \zeta = \frac{\log(4\sqrt{\rho^2 + (1+\kappa)d}\|\vb_{mm}\|^3  d\rho^2 )}{R\Xi} .
        \]
        \item The label margin induced by $\vb_{(r, R)}/r$ in \textit{v-SVM} is at least $(1-\gamma)\Gamma$, where $\gamma = \frac{2\sqrt{\rho^2 + (1+\kappa)d}}{\Gamma \exp((1 - \zeta) R\Xi)}$.
    \end{itemize}   
\end{restatable}
Here, $(\zeta, \gamma)$ quantify the difference between $(\pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r, R)})$ and $(\pb_{mm}, \vb_{mm})$. As $R \rightarrow \infty$, both $\zeta$ and $\gamma$ converge to 0. Thus, for sufficiently large $R$, we conclude that $\pb_{(r, R)}^{\top} (\bmu_k - \bxi_i)$ 
becomes large for $i \in \itc_k$. This ensures that $\pb_{(r, R)}$ captures sufficient information about signal tokens, which enhances the accuracy of test sample predictions. Specifically, the attention weight on a signal token is lower bounded by
$
0.5 (1 - \zeta)R\Xi \le \la \pb_{(r, R)}, \bmu_j \ra
$.
Since the signal token remains invariant between training and test data, we can estimate the attention layer's output for a new test sample $(\bX, y)$.
\begin{lemma}
    Suppose that Assumption \ref{cond: main_cond_f} holds, with probability at least $1-\delta$ on the training dataset, for a given test sample \((\bX, y)\) with \(\bX = (\bmu^{\star}, \bxi^{\star})\), where the signal \(\bmu^{\star}\) can be \(\bmu_1\) or \(\bmu_2\), we have with probability at least $1 - \exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{2} (1 - \zeta)\Xi - K/R)^2 \big)$ that
    $\la \pb_{(r, R)}, \bmu^{\star} \ra - \la \pb_{(r, R)}, \bxi^{\star} \ra \ge K$,
    for $K \le \frac{1}{2} (1 - \zeta)R\Xi$. Here $\zeta, \Xi$ follow the definitions in Theorem \ref{thrm: finite_converge}.
\end{lemma}
Therefore, if $K$ is large, which is equivalent to $R$ is large, the attention weight on the signal token is much greater than the noise token. As a result, the signal token $\bmu^{\star}$ will dominate the attention layer's output, i.e. $\rb^{\star} \rightarrow \bmu^{\star}$.

Finally, from Theorem \ref{thrm: finite_converge}, $\vb_{(r, R)}$ converges to $\vb_{mm}$, ensuring that it can make accurate predictions on $(\bmu_k, y)$ if $(\bmu_k, y)$ comes from the clean set. %By setting $K = \log(n \sqrt{1/\rho^2 + \eta n/d}) + \log(\log(6n^2)+d) + C < \frac{1}{2} (1 - \zeta)R\Xi$, 
Thus, w.h.p. the learning of signal token $y \cdot \la \vb_{(r, R)}, \bmu^{\star} \ra$ is large enough to eliminate the randomness introduced by the noise token (denoted by $\Delta(\bxi^{\star})$ here) and the model will make accurate prediction with high probability:
$y \cdot f(\pb_{(r, R)}, \vb_{(r, R)}; \bX) \approx y \cdot \vb_{(r, R)}^{\top} \bmu^{\star} - \Delta(\bxi^{\star}) \ge 0$.


\section{Experiments}


\begin{figure}[t]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.49\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{gd_2_iterations/acc.pdf}
        \label{fig:small-ss-acc}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.49\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{gd_2_iterations/sm.pdf}
        \label{fig:small-ss-sm-prob}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{The left panel shows the train and test accuracies during training. It shows that benign overfitting occurs after $2$ iterations. After the first iteration, the model correctly classifies the clean training examples, but not the noisy ones. In the right panel, we show the softmax probability of the signal token for clean and noisy samples (average of the softmax probabilities $s_{j,1}^{t}$ over $\clean$ and $\noise$ respectively).
    We see that after $2$ iterations, the attention focuses on signal tokens for clean examples, and on noise tokens for noisy examples. 
    Parameters: $n=200, d=40000, \beta = 0.025, \rho = 30, \eta = 0.05, \text{test sample size} = 2000$.}
    \label{fig:accuracies_large_step_size}
\end{figure}


We complement our theoretical results with an empirical study on benign overfitting in single-head softmax attention.
We trained single-head softmax attention models (Eq.~(\ref{eq:model})) on data generated as specified in Section~\ref{sec:data.generation} using GD with a fixed step size and the logistic loss function. In all figures, the x-axis corresponds to the time and has a log scale. We add $1$ to the time so that the initialization $t = 0$ can be shown in the log scale (i.e. iteration $10^0$ is the initialization). 

In Figure \ref{fig:accuracies_large_step_size}, we consider a setting similar to Theorem~\ref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration}, and demonstrate that benign overfitting occurs after two iterations, and that the behavior of GD aligns with our discussion in Remark~\ref{rem:GD.behavior}. We also plot how the softmax probabilities evolve during training, and see after two iterations a behavior similar to the first item of Theorem~\ref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration}. In Figure~\ref{fig:accuracies_small_step_size}, we consider a similar setting, but with a smaller step size. Here, benign overfitting occurs after about $150$ iterations.

In Figure~\ref{fig:different-snr}, we explore the behavior of GD with different SNR levels. When the SNR is too small the model exhibits catastrophic overfitting, namely, it fits the training data but has trivial generalization performance. When the SNR is sufficiently large we observe benign overfitting.
In Figure~\ref{fig:different-dim}, we investigate the overfitting behavior with different dimensions $d$. If $d$ is sufficiently large we observe benign overfitting. If it is very small we are not able to overfit, namely, the training accuracy does not reach $1$. For intermediate values of $d$ we observe harmful overfitting.
Thus, we see that high dimensionality is crucial for benign overfitting.
Interestingly, we can see that achieving benign overfitting is possible even when $d \ll n^2$, suggesting that our assumption on $d$ in the theoretical results might not be tight.

\begin{figure}[hb]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.49\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{gd_1000_iterations/acc.pdf}
        \label{fig:large-ss-acc}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hfill
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.49\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{gd_1000_iterations/sm.pdf}
        \label{fig:large-ss-sm-prob}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{The left panel shows train and test accuracies during training with a small step size. The clean training samples are correctly classified already after one iteration, but in contrast to Theorem~\ref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration} and Figure~\ref{fig:accuracies_large_step_size}, benign overfitting occurs after about $150$ iterations.
    In the right panel we see that the attention starts separating signal and noise tokens shortly before benign overfitting occurs.
    Parameters: $n=200, d=40000, \beta = 0.0001, \rho = 30, \eta = 0.05, \text{test sample size} = 2000$.}
    \label{fig:accuracies_small_step_size}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[!h]
    \centering
    \begin{subfigure}[b]{0.49\textwidth}
        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{different_rhos/differents_rhos.pdf}
        \caption{Accuracy - different SNR's}
        \label{fig:different-snr}
    \end{subfigure}
    \hfill

        \centering
        \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{different_rhos/differents_dimmensions.pdf}
        \caption{Accuracy - different dimenssions}
        \label{fig:different-dim}
    \end{subfigure}
    \caption{Comparing train (solid lines) and test (dashed lines) accuracies, with different SNR (left panel) and different dimensions (right panel). In the left panel, we observe that for small SNR (purple line), the model exhibits catastrophic overfitting, similar to Theorem~\ref{thrm: main_thrm_mm_s}. For larger SNR values, the model demonstrates benign overfitting. 
    In the right panel, we see that for small $d$ (purple line), the model is unable to fit the data (at least in the first $10^5$ first iterations), and both the train and test accuracies are at the noise-rate level. For intermediate values of $d$ (green and blue lines), the model exhibits harmful overfitting, and for larger $d$ (yellow line) %(which still scales with $n$), 
    the model exhibits benign overfitting. We note that benign overfitting occurs here for $d=2n \ll n^2$, which suggests that the assumptions on $d$ in our theorems are loose.
    Parameters (left panel): $n=400, d=40000, \beta = 0.00015, \eta = 0.1, \text{test sample size} = 2000$. Parameters (right panel): $n=500, \beta = 0.02, \rho = 30, \eta = 0.1, \text{test sample size} = 10000$.}
    \label{fig:differents_rho_and_dimmension}
\end{figure}




\section{Conclusion}


This paper took an initial step in establishing the benign overfitting phenomenon in a single-head softmax attention model.
Our results open up several future directions, including analyzing gradient descent for more than 2 steps, more complex data distributions containing more than 2 tokens and varying sequence length, and the self-attention architecture.

\section{Appendix}

\begingroup
\parindent=0em
\etocsettocstyle{\rule{\linewidth}{\tocrulewidth}\vskip0.5\baselineskip}{\rule{\linewidth}{\tocrulewidth}}
\localtableofcontents

\begin{remark} \label{remark: original_data_setting}
    Throughout our proofs, we assume without loss of generality that $\bmu_1 = (\rho, 0, 0, ..., 0)^{\top}$, $\bmu_2 = (0, \rho, 0, ..., 0)^{\top}$ and $\bxi_i = (0, 0, \bxi^{\top})$ for $\bxi \sim \mathcal{N}(\zero, \bI_{d-2})$. Indeed, since $\bmu_1$ and $\bmu_2$ are orthogonal, we can find orthogonal matrix $\bA \in \R^{d \times d}$ such that $\bA\bmu_1 =  (\rho, 0, 0, ..., 0)^{\top}, \bA\bmu_2 = (0, \rho, 0, ..., 0)^{\top}$ and $\bA\bxi_i \sim \mathcal{N}(\bA\zero, \bA(\bI_{d} - \bmu_1\bmu_1^{\top}/\rho^2 -  \bmu_2\bmu_2^{\top}/\rho^2)\bA^{\top} )$, which mean that $\bA\bxi_i = (0, 0, \bxi^{\top})$ for $\bxi \sim \mathcal{N}(\zero, \bI_{d-2})$. 
We emphasize that an orthogonal 
transformation does not affect our results.
\end{remark}


\subsection{Proofs for Sec. \ref{sec: gd}}


\subsubsection{Notations for Sec. \ref{sec: gd}.}  Given $a,b,c \in \R$, we denote by $c(a \pm b)$ the close segment $[c(a-b),c(a+b)]$. Given vector $\bx$, we denote by $\bx[i]$ the $i^{\text{th}}$ coordinate of $\bx$, and $\bx[i:j]$ denotes the subvector containing the elements from the $i^{\text{th}}$ to the $j^{\text{th}}$, inclusive.
We also list some key notations used in this section for convenience.

\begin{table}[h!]
\caption{Usefull notation.}
\label{table:1}
\begin{center} 
\begin{tabular}{ c c }
 $\bx_{i,j}$ & $j^{\text{th}}$ token in the $i^{\text{th}}$ sample \\   
  $\bgamma_{i,j}^{t}$    & $y_i\vb_t^{\top}\bx_{i,j}$ i.e. $j^{\text{th}}$ token score in time $t$ \\  
 $\alpha_{i,j}^t$  & softmax probability of the $j^{\text{th}}$ token in the $i^{\text{th}}$ sample in time $t$ \\
 $\ell_{t,i}$ & $\ell(\bX_i;\vb_t,\pb_t)$
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{table}


We remind that $\clean, \noise \subseteq [n]$ denotes the indices of clean and noisy training examples, and  $\clean_k, \noise_k$ denotes the clean and noisy examples from cluster $k \in \{1,2\}$. For example if $i \in \clean_1$, then $x_{i,1} = \mu_1$ and $y_1 = 1$, and for $j \in \noise_1$  we have that $x_{j,1} = \mu_1$ and $y_1 = -1$. Let $\SM'(\vb) := \nabla \SM (\vb) = \diag(\SM(\vb)) - \SM(\vb)\SM(\vb)^{\top}$ denote the Jacobian of the softmax function $\SM(\vb)$ at $\vb \in \R^d$.



\subsubsection{Additional Lemmas \& Definitions for Sec \ref{sec: gd}.}
The following equations will be useful throughout the proof:
\begin{align}
    &\nabla_{\vb} \Lcal(\vb,\pb) = \frac{1}{n} \sumn \ell_i' \cdot  y_i\bX_i^{\top} \SM (X_i\pb)  \label{eq: loss_gradient_by_v} \\
    &\nabla_{\pb} \Lcal(\vb,\pb) = \frac{1}{n} \sumn \ell_i' \cdot  \bX_i^{\top} \SM' (X_i\pb)  \bgamma_i, \text{ \ where \ } \bgamma_i = y_i\vb^{\top}\bX_{i} \label{eq: loss_gradient_by_p}\\
    & \ell'(x) = -1/(1+\exp(x)) \label{eq: loss_derevative}\\
    & \SM'(\vb) = \diag(\SM(\vb)) - \SM(\vb)\SM(\vb)^{\top} \label{eq: softmax_gradient}
\end{align} 

\begin{definition} [Good Training Set] \label{def: good_training_set}
We say that a training set $(\bX_1,\dots,\bX_n)$ is \emph{good} if 
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\|\bxi_i\|_2^2 \in (1 \pm o_n(1))d$, for all $i \in [n]$.
    \item $|\la \bxi_i, \bxi_j \ra | \le \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}$, for any $i,j \in [n]$. 
     \item $|\noise_k| \in \frac{n}{2} (\eta \pm o_n(1))$  and $|\clean_k| = \frac{n}{2} (1-\eta \pm o_n(1))$, for $k \in \{1,2\}$. 
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition} [Good Test Sample] \label{def: good_test_sample}
We say that a test sample $(\bX = (\bx_1,\bx_2), y)$ is \emph{good} w.r.t. a training set $(\bX_1,\dots,\bX_n)$ and constant $C_1$ if 
\begin{align*}
    |\la \bx_{i,2}, \bx_2 \ra | \le  \frac{d}{C_1 n}, \ \ \ \forall i \in [n] 
\end{align*}
\end{definition}

Next we write Lemma \ref{lem: concen_samplesize} slightly different, and also add a formal proof for completeness:
\begin{lemma} \label{lemma: estimate-number-of-noisy/clean-samples}
    Let $\delta > 0$ and $C>0$. Suppose that Assumption ~\ref{assumption: gd} (item \ref{assumption: gd-number-of-samples-is-big}) holds with constant $C$, then with probability at least $1-\delta/2$ we have that 
    \begin{align*}
        \left|\clean_k\right| \in \frac{n}{2}(1-\eta \pm \sqrt{2/C}), \ \ \  \left|\noise_k\right| \in \frac{n}{2}(\eta \pm \sqrt{2/C}), \ \ \  \forall k \in \{1,2\}.
    \end{align*}
Moreover, 
we have
\begin{align*}
       \left|\clean_k\right| \in \frac{n}{2}(1-\eta \pm o_n(1)), \ \ \  \left|\noise_k\right| \in \frac{n}{2}(\eta \pm o_n(1)), \ \ \  \forall k \in \{1,2\}~.
\end{align*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By Hoeffding's inequality, 
    \begin{align*}
         \mathbb{P} \left(\left| |\clean_j| -\frac{n}{2}(1-\eta) \right| \geq \sqrt{n \log(16/\delta) / 2}  \right) \leq \delta / 8, 
    \end{align*}
which means that with probability at least $1-\delta/8$ we have that $|\clean_j| \in \frac{n}{2} (1 - \eta \pm c_n)$, where $c_n = \sqrt{2n \log(16/\delta)}/n$. Hence, if $n \geq C\log(16/\delta)$, then $c_n = \sqrt{2 \log(16/\delta)}/\sqrt{n} \leq \sqrt{2/C}$. Similarly, we can estimate $|\noise_k|$ for $k \in \{1,2\}$, and by union bound, the result follows.
\end{proof}


\begin{lemma} \label{lemma: soft-max-derivative}
Let $\bz,\bgamma,\pb \in \R^2$ and let $ \balpha = \SM(\pb)$, then
\begin{align*}
    \bz^T \SM'(\pb)\bgamma = (\gamma_1 - \gamma_2)(1-\alpha_1)\alpha_1(z_1-z_2)
\end{align*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Observe that $ \alpha_1 + \alpha_2 = 1$. Therefore,
     \begin{align*}
         \bz^T \SM'(\pb)\bgamma &= \bz^T \diag(\balpha)\bgamma - \bz^T \balpha \balpha^{\top} \bgamma = \sum_{i=1}^2 z_i\alpha_i\gamma_i - \sum_{i=1}^2 \alpha_iz_i \sum_{i=1}^2 \alpha_i \gamma_i \\
         &=  z_1\alpha_1\gamma_1 + z_2\alpha_2\gamma_2 - (\alpha_1z_1 + \alpha_2z_2) (\alpha_1\gamma_1 + \alpha_2\gamma_2) \\
         &=   \left(\gamma_2 - (\alpha_1\gamma_1 + \alpha_2\gamma_2) \right)\alpha_2z_2 +  \left(\gamma_1 -(\alpha_1\gamma_1 + \alpha_2\gamma_2) \right)\alpha_1z_1 \\
         &= \left(\alpha_1 \gamma_2 - \alpha_1\gamma_1 \right)\alpha_2z_2 + \left(\alpha_2\gamma_1 - \alpha_2\gamma_2 \right)\alpha_1z_1 \\
         &= -\alpha_1\left( \gamma_1 - \gamma_2 \right)\alpha_2z_2 + \alpha_2\left(\gamma_1 - \gamma_2 \right)\alpha_1z_1 \\
         & = \alpha_1\left( \gamma_1 - \gamma_2 \right)\alpha_2(z_1 - z_2)
     \end{align*}
\end{proof}
Lemma \ref{lemma: soft-max-derivative} allows us to analyze $\nabla_{\bp} \Lcal$ as a function of the score gap.
\subsubsection{Proof of Thm. \ref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration}}
\begin{proof} [Proof of Thm. \ref{thm: gd-after-2-iteration}]
To simplify the proof, we will use the following assumption, which is slightly weaker than Assumption \ref{assumption: gd_main}:
\begin{assumption} [Assumptions for GD with SNR = $\Theta(1/\sqrt{n})$] \label{assumption: gd}
Let $\delta > 0$ be a desired probability of failure.
For constants $\signalconst \geq 6, \stepSizeConst \geq 16\signalconst\log(\signalconst^2)$, there exists some large enough constant $C = C(\stepSizeConst)$, such that the following hold:
    \begin{enumerate}
        \item Number of samples $n$ should be sufficiently large: $n \geq C\log(16/\delta)$ \label{assumption: gd-number-of-samples-is-big}
        \item Dimension $d$ should be sufficiently large: $d \ge C n^2 \log(12n^2/\delta)$. \label{assumption: gd-high-dimmension}
        \item Signal strength is: $\rho = \signalconst\sqrt{d/n}$ \label{assumption: gd-signal-strength}
        \item Label flipping rate $\eta$: $\eta \leq 1/C$. \label{assumption: gd-label-flipping-rate}
        \item The step size $\beta$ satisfies: $\beta = (\stepSizeConst \cdot n)/ (\signalconst^2 \cdot d)$. \label{assumption: gd-step-size}
        \item Initialization at zero: $\norm{\bv_0} = \norm{\bp_0} = 0$. \label{assumption: gd-initialization-at-zero}
    \end{enumerate}
\end{assumption}
Apart from slight adjustments to the constants within the logarithm at items \ref{assumption: gd-number-of-samples-is-big} and \ref{assumption: gd-high-dimmension} (which can be absorbed into $C$), the only changes are  $\stepSizeConst \geq 16\signalconst\log(\signalconst^2)$ (instead of $C_{\beta} \geq 16$) and $\beta = (\stepSizeConst \cdot n)/ (\signalconst^2 \cdot d)$ (instead of $\beta = C_{\beta} \cdot (n/d)$). Indeed, given $C_{\beta} \geq 16, C_{\rho} \geq 6$ and $\beta = C_{\beta} \cdot (n/d) $ which satisfy Assumption \ref{assumption: gd_main}, define $\signalconst := C_{\rho} \geq 6, \stepSizeConst := C_{\beta}\signalconst^2 \geq 16\signalconst\log(\signalconst^2)$, which holds for any $\signalconst \geq 6$. We also have that $\beta = C_{\beta} \cdot(n/d) = (\stepSizeConst/\signalconst^2)\cdot(n/d)$ , i.e., $\beta, \signalconst, \stepSizeConst$ satisfy Assumption \ref{assumption: gd}. 

Next, under Assumption \ref{assumption: gd}, we argue that with probability at least $1 - \delta$ the training set is good (Def. \ref{def: good_training_set}) i.e.:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $|\clean_k| \in \frac{n}{2} (\eta \pm o_n(1))$  and $\noise_k \in \frac{n}{2} (1-\eta \pm o_n(1))$, for $k \in \{1,2\}$.
    \item  $\|\bxi_i\|_2^2 \in (1 \pm o_n(1))d$, for any $i \in [n]$.
    \item $|\la \bxi_i, \bxi_j \ra | \le \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}$, for any $i,j \in [n]$. 
\end{itemize}
Indeed, this holds by Lemma~\ref{lemma: noise_balance}, Lemma~\ref{lemma: estimate-number-of-noisy/clean-samples}, and the union bound. 
We emphasize that the notation $o_n(1)$ represents a term that becomes arbitrarily small as $n$ increases, and thus it can be bounded by a small constant if $C$ from Assumption~\ref{assumption: gd-number-of-samples-is-big} is large enough.



Next, we show that under a good training set, the model exhibits benign overfitting, already after two iterations. See Remark \ref{remark: original_data_setting} for the data setting used throughout the proof.

  




\textbf{GD after 1 iteration.}
We start by analyzing the first coordinate of $\bv_1$ (i.e. $\bv$ after one iteration of GD). By assumption \ref{assumption: gd} ( item\ref{assumption: gd-initialization-at-zero}), we have that $\bp_0 = \bv_0 = \zero$, which implies that $\ell_{0,i}' = -1/2$, for any $i \in [n]$. Hence
\begin{align*}
    -\steps \nabla_{\vb} \Lcal(\vb_0,\pb_0)[1] &= -\frac{\steps }{2n} \sumn \ell_{0,i}' \cdot  y_i\bx_{i,1}[1] = \frac{\steps }{4n} \sum_{i\in \clean_1} y_i \rho + \frac{\steps }{4n} \sum_{i\in \noise_1} y_i \rho \\
     &= \frac{\steps }{4n} (|\clean_1| - |\noise_1| )\rho  \\
     &\in  \frac{\steps }{8} (1-2\eta \pm o_n(1))\rho &&\text{``good'' training set}
\end{align*}
In the same way, we can estimate the second coordinate of $\vb_{t=1}$:
\begin{align*}
    \vb_{t=1}[2] = \frac{\steps }{4n} \sum_{i\in \clean_2} y_i \rho + \frac{\steps }{4n} \sum_{i\in \noise_2} y_i \rho \in  -\frac{\steps }{8} (1-2\eta\pm o_n(1))\rho,
\end{align*}
where we remind that $y_i = -1$, when $i \in \clean_2$, hence $\vb_{t=1}[2]$ has the same bounds as $\vb_{t=1}[1]$, just with opposite sign.
We move to analyze the rest of the coordinates of $\vb_{t=1}$:
\begin{align*}
    \vb_{t}[3:d] =  \frac{\steps }{4n} \sumn y_i \bxi_i.
\end{align*}
Overall, we can write $\vb_{t=1}$ as $\lambda_1^{t=1} \bmu_1 + \lambda_2^{t=1} \bmu_2 + \sumn y_i\theta_i^{t=1}\bxi_i$ with
\begin{align} 
    &\lambda_1^{t=1} \in  \frac{\steps }{8 } (1-2\eta\pm o_n(1)), \ \ \lambda_2^{t=1} \in -\frac{\steps }{8 } (1-2\eta\pm o_n(1)), \ \ \theta_i^{t=1} =  \frac{\steps }{4n}. \label{eq: gd_v1_coefficients}
\end{align}
Moreover, since $\bgamma_{i}^{t=0} = \zero$ for every $i \in [n]$, we have that $ \bp_1 = \zero$ (see Eq. \ref{eq: loss_gradient_by_p}). 

 \textbf{Preparation for next iteration.} To estimate $(\vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2})$, we first need to estimate the loss for clean/noisy samples and the score difference, i.e. $\gamma_{i,1}^{1} - \gamma_{i,2}^{1}, i \in \clean$ and $\gamma_{j,2}^{1} - \gamma_{j,1}^{1}, j \in \noise$. 

We remind that $\norm{\bmu_j}^2 = \rho^2 = \signalconst^2 d / n$ (Assumption \ref{assumption: gd} (item \ref{assumption: gd-signal-strength})).
For $j \in \clean_k$, where $k \in \{1,2\}$ we have that
\begin{align}
    y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) &= \frac{1}{2}y_j\vb_{t=1}^{\top}(\bx_{j,1}+\bx_{j,2}) \notag &&\text{since \ } \bp_1 = \zero\\
    &=  \frac{1}{2}|\lambda_k^{t=1}|\norm{\bmu_k}^2 +  \frac{1}{2}\theta_j^{t=1}\norm{\bxi_j}^2 + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in[n]: i\neq j} y_iy_j \theta_j^{t=1}\bxi_i^{\top}\bxi_j &&y_j\lambda_k^{t=1} > 0 
\end{align}
Since the training set is ``good'' then by Eq.~\ref{eq: gd_v1_coefficients}, we can bound $ y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1})$ as follows:
\begin{align}
    y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) &\leq  \frac{\steps }{16 } (1-2\eta + o_n(1)) \cdot \signalconst^2 \cdot \frac{d}{n} + \frac{\steps }{8n} d (1 + o_n(1)) + \frac{\steps }{8n} n  \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}\notag \\
    &\leq  \left(\frac{\signalconst^2 (1-2\eta) +2 + o_n(1)}{16}\right) \cdot \frac{\beta d}{n}  \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{Assumption \ref{assumption: gd} (item \ref{assumption: gd-high-dimmension})} \notag \\
    &=    \stepSizeConst \cdot \left(\frac{(1-2\eta) +2/\signalconst^2 + o_n(1)}{16}\right) \notag  \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{Assumption \ref{assumption: gd} (item \ref{assumption: gd-step-size})} \\
    &\leq  \frac{1.1 \stepSizeConst }{16}, \label{eq: gd_margin_for_clean_sampe_t=1_upper_bound}
\end{align}
 where the last inequality holds since $\signalconst \geq 5$, which implies that $2/\signalconst^2 + o_n(1) \leq 0.1$. Similarly, we have that
\begin{align}
    y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) &\geq  \frac{\steps }{16 } (1-2\eta - o_n(1)) \cdot \signalconst^2 \cdot \frac{d}{n} + \frac{\steps }{8n} d (1 - o_n(1)) - \frac{\steps }{8n} n  \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}\notag \\
    &\geq  \left(\frac{\signalconst^2 (1-2\eta) +2 - o_n(1)}{16}\right) \cdot \frac{\beta d}{n} \notag \\
    &= \stepSizeConst \cdot \left(\frac{ (1-2\eta) +2/\signalconst^2 - o_n(1)}{16}\right)  \notag \\
    &\geq  \frac{0.9 \stepSizeConst }{16} \label{eq: gd_margin_for_clean_sampe_t=1_lower_bound} 
\end{align}

For $j \in \noise_k$ we have:
\begin{align*}
    y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) &= \frac{1}{2}y_j\vb_{t=1}^{\top}(\bx_{j,1}+\bx_{j,2}) \notag &&\text{since \ } \bp_1 = \zero\\
    &= - \frac{1}{2}|\lambda_k^{t=1}|\norm{\bmu_k}^2 + \frac{1}{2}\theta_j^{t=1} \norm{\bxi_j}^2 + \frac{\steps }{8n} \sum_{i\in[n]: i\neq j} y_iy_j \bxi_i^{\top}\bxi_j &&y_j\lambda_k^{t=1} < 0\notag \\
\end{align*}
Since the training set is good then by Eq. \ref{eq: gd_v1_coefficients}, we can bound $ y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1})$ as follows:
\begin{align}
    y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) &\leq - \frac{\steps }{16 } (1-2\eta - o_n(1)) \cdot \signalconst^2 \cdot \frac{d}{n} + \frac{\steps }{8n} d (1 + o_n(1)) + \frac{\steps }{8n} n  \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}\notag \\
    &\leq  \left(\frac{-\signalconst^2 (1-2\eta) +2 + o_n(1)}{16}\right) \cdot \frac{\beta d}{n} \notag\\
    & = \stepSizeConst \cdot \left(\frac{- (1-2\eta) +2/\signalconst^2 + o_n(1)}{16}\right)  \notag\\
    &\leq   \frac{-0.9 \stepSizeConst }{16} , \label{eq: gd_margin_for_noise_sampe_t=1_upper_bound}
\end{align}
 where the last inequality holds for small enough $\eta$ and since $\signalconst \geq 5$, which implies that $2/\signalconst^2 + 2\eta+ o_n(1) \leq 0.1$. Similarly, we have that
\begin{align}
    y_jf(\bX_j;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1}) &\geq  -\frac{\steps }{16 } (1-2\eta + o_n(1)) \cdot \signalconst^2 \cdot \frac{d}{n} + \frac{\steps }{8n} d (1 - o_n(1)) - \frac{\steps }{8n} n  \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}\notag \\
    &\geq  \left(\frac{-\signalconst^2 (1-2\eta) +2 - o_n(1)}{16}\right) \cdot \frac{\beta d}{n} \notag\\
    & \geq \stepSizeConst\left(\frac{-(1-2\eta) +2/\signalconst^2  - o_n(1)}{16}\right)   \notag \\
    &\geq   \frac{-1.1 \stepSizeConst }{16}~. \label{eq: gd_margin_for_noise_sampe_t=1_lower_bound}
\end{align}

We remind that $-\ell_{1,j}' =1/(1 + \exp(y_if(\bX_i;\vb_{t=1},\pb_{t=1})))$ and that $\beta = \stepSizeConst \cdot n/(d\signalconst^2)$ for some constant $\stepSizeConst \geq 16\signalconst$. Combine with Eqs. \ref{eq: gd_margin_for_clean_sampe_t=1_upper_bound} and \ref{eq: gd_margin_for_clean_sampe_t=1_lower_bound}, we have that
\begin{align}
    &i\in \clean, \ \ -\ell_{t=1,i}' \geq  1/(1 + \exp(1.1\stepSizeConst/16)) :=  m_{\clean}^{t=1} > 0  \label{eq: gd_loss_clean_t=1_lower_bound}\\
    &i\in \clean, \ \ -\ell_{t=1,i}' \leq  1/(1 + \exp(0.9\stepSizeConst/16))  := M_{\clean}^{t=1} \leq 1/(4\signalconst^2), \label{eq: gd_loss_clean_t=1_upper_bound}
\end{align}
where the last inequality holds since $\stepSizeConst \geq 16\signalconst$ and since $1 + \exp(0.9\signalconst) \geq 4\signalconst^2$ for any $\signalconst \geq 6$.

Moreover, by Eqs.~\ref{eq: gd_margin_for_noise_sampe_t=1_upper_bound} and~\ref{eq: gd_margin_for_noise_sampe_t=1_lower_bound}, we have that
\begin{align}
    &j\in \noise, \ \ -\ell_{t=1,j}' \geq 1/(1+\exp ( -0.9\stepSizeConst/16)) := m_{\noise}^{t=1} \geq 0.99 \label{eq: gd_loss_noise_t=1_lower_bound} \\
    &j\in \noise, \ \ -\ell_{t=1,j}' \leq  1/(1+\exp ( -1.1\stepSizeConst/16)) : = M_{\noise}^{t=1} \leq 1 \label{eq: gd_loss_noise_t=1_upper_bound} 
\end{align}
The notations $M_{\clean}^{t}$ and $m_{\clean}^{t}$ ($M_{\noise}^{t}$ and $m_{\noise}^{t}$) denote the upper and lower bounds, respectively, on the derivative of the loss for clean (noisy) samples at time $t$, and we use them throughout the proof. We remind that $\gamma_{i,j}^{t} = y_i\vb_t^{\top}\bx_{i,j}$. Then
by Eq. \ref{eq: gd_v1_coefficients}, for $i \in \clean_k$ we have that
\begin{align} 
    &\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}  \in \frac{\beta}{8}(1-2\eta \pm o_n(1)) \rho^2 = \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1-2\eta \pm o_n(1)) \notag \\ 
    & \gamma_{i,2}^{t=1} \in  \frac{\beta d}{4n}(1 \pm o_n(1)) = \frac{\stepSizeConst }{4}(1/\signalconst^2 \pm o_n(1)) \notag \\
    &%\Longrightarrow 
    \gamma_{i,1}^{t=1} - \gamma_{i,2}^{t=2} \in \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 -2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta \pm o_n(1))~.
    \label{eq: score_clean_t=1}
\end{align}
where in the calculation of $\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1}$ we use  $\sum_{i\in[n]: i\neq j} y_iy_j \theta_j^{t=1}\bxi_i^{\top}\bxi_j = o_n(1) \cdot d$, since the training set is good.
For $i \in \noise_k$, we have that
\begin{align}
     &\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1} \in -\frac{\beta}{8}(1-2\eta \pm o_n(1)) \rho^2 = -\frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1-2\eta \pm o_n(1)) \notag \\ 
    & \gamma_{i,2}^{t=1} \in  \frac{\beta d}{4n}(1 \pm o_n(1)) = \frac{\stepSizeConst }{4}(1/\signalconst^2 \pm o_n(1)) \notag \\
    &%\Longrightarrow 
    \gamma_{i,2}^{t=1} - \gamma_{i,1}^{t=2} \in \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta \pm o_n(1))~.
 \label{eq: score_noise_t=1}
\end{align}

                             
\textbf{GD after 2 iterations.} \\
\textbf{Analysis of $\bv_{t=2}$}. \\
Observe that
\begin{align*}
 -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1) =  -\frac{\steps }{n} \sumn \ell_{1,i}' \cdot  y_i\bX_i^{\top} \SM (X_i\pb_1) = -\frac{\steps }{2n} \sumn \ell_{1,i}' \cdot  y_i(\bx_{i,1} + \bx_{i,2}). 
\end{align*}
We start by analyzing the first coordinate of $\nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)$. 
\begin{align}  
    -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)[1] &= \frac{\steps }{2n} \sum_{i \in \clean_1} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot  y_i\bx_{i,1}[1] + \frac{\steps }{2n} \sum_{i \in \noise_1} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot  y_i\bx_{i,1}[1] \notag \\
    &= \frac{\steps }{2n} \sum_{i \in \clean_1} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot  \rho - \frac{\steps }{2n} \sum_{i \in \noise_1} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot  \rho \notag \\
     &= \frac{\steps }{2n} \left(\sum_{i \in \clean_1} -\ell_{1,i}' -  \sum_{j \in \noise_1} -\ell_{1,j}' \right) \cdot  \rho~.  \label{eq: gradient_of_v[1]_t=2}
\end{align}
Observe that
\begin{align*}
   \sum_{i \in \clean_k} -\ell_{1,i}' -  \sum_{j \in \noise_k} -\ell_{1,j}' &\geq \frac{n}{2}(1-\eta - o_n(1)) \cdot m_{\clean} -\frac{n}{2}(\eta + o_n(1))\cdot M_{\noise} &&\text{good training set} \\
   & >  0 &&\text{Eqs. \ref{eq: gd_loss_clean_t=1_lower_bound} and \ref{eq: gd_loss_noise_t=1_upper_bound}},
\end{align*}
where the last inequality holds for small enough $\eta \leq 1/C$, where $C := C(\signalconst,\stepSizeConst)$ (see Assumption \ref{assumption: gd-label-flipping-rate}).
Substituting it into Eq. \ref{eq: gradient_of_v[1]_t=2}, we obtain that
\begin{align*}
     -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)[1] > 0.
\end{align*}
On the other hand, by Eq. \ref{eq: gradient_of_v[1]_t=2}, we can upper bound the first coordinate of the gradient of $\vb$ by 
\begin{align*}
    -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)[1] &\leq \frac{\steps }{2n} \left(\sum_{i \in \clean_1} -\ell_{1,i}' \right) \cdot  \rho\\
    &\leq \frac{\steps }{17} \cdot  \rho &&-\ell_{1,i}' < 1/16, \text{Eq. \ref{eq: gd_loss_clean_t=1_upper_bound}}.
\end{align*}
Similarly, we can estimate the second coordinate of $\nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)$:
\begin{align*}
0 \geq -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)[2] \geq -\frac{\steps }{17} \cdot  \rho. 
\end{align*}
Write $\vb_{t=2} = \lambda_1^{t=2} \bmu_1 + \lambda_2^{t=2} \bmu_2 + \sumn y_i\theta_i^{t=2}\bxi_i$. Together with Eq. \ref{eq: gd_v1_coefficients}, we get that
\begin{align} 
    &\lambda_1^{t=2} = \lambda_1^{t=1}  -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)[1] / \rho \leq \frac{\beta}{8}(1 + o_n(1)) + \frac{\beta}{17} \leq \frac{3\beta}{16} \label{eq: gd_upper_bound_lam1_t=2}\\
    &\lambda_1^{t=2} \geq \lambda_1^{t=1} \geq \frac{\beta}{8}(1-2\eta - o_n(1)) \label{eq: gd_lower_bound_lam1_t=2}\\
     &\lambda_2^{t=2} = \lambda_2^{t=1}  -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)[2] \geq -\frac{\beta}{8}(1 + o_n(1)) - \frac{\beta}{17} \geq -\frac{3\beta}{16} \label{eq: gd_lower_bound_lam2_t=2}\\
    &\lambda_2^{t=2} \leq \lambda_2^{t=1} \leq -\frac{\beta}{8}(1-2\eta - o_n(1))~. \label{eq: gd_upper_bound_lam2_t=2}
\end{align}





Next, we analyze the rest of the coordinates of $\nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)$. 
\begin{align*}
    -\steps \nabla_{\bv} \Lcal (\bv_1,\bp_1)[3:d] &= \frac{\steps }{2n} \sum_{i \in \clean} -\ell_{1,i}'  \cdot  y_i\bxi_i + \frac{\steps }{2n} \sum_{j \in \noise} -\ell_{1,j}' \cdot  y_j\bxi_j,
    \end{align*}
and use it to analyze the coefficients of the noise (second) tokens in $\bv_{t=2}$, i.e., $\theta_i^{t=2}$. 
Indeed, for $i \in \clean$ we have that
\begin{align}
    \theta_i^{t=2} &= \theta_i^{t=1} - \frac{\beta}{2n} \ell_{1,i}' = \frac{\beta}{2n}(-\ell_{1,i}' + 0.5) &&\text{Eq. \ref{eq: gd_v1_coefficients}} \notag\\
    &\in \left[\frac{\beta}{2n}(m_{\clean} + 0.5),\frac{\beta}{2n}(M_{\clean} + 0.5)\right] 
    \label{eq: gd_bound_theta_clean_t=2}.
\end{align}
For $j \in \noise$ we have that
\begin{align}
    \theta_j^{t=2} &= \theta_j^{t=1} - \frac{\beta}{2n} \ell_{1,j}' = \frac{\beta}{2n}(-\ell_{1,j}' + 0.5) &&\text{Eq. \ref{eq: gd_v1_coefficients}} \notag\\
    &\in \left[\frac{\beta}{2n}(m_{\noise} + 0.5),\frac{\beta}{2n}(M_{\noise} + 0.5)\right] \label{eq: gd_bound_theta_noise_t=2}.
\end{align}
Now we move to analyze $\bp_{t=2}$ and show that $\bp_{t=2}$ focuses on optimal tokens for training samples.


\textbf{$\bp_{t=2}$ focuses on optimal tokens}.
Observe that $\bp_2 = -\steps  \nabla_{\pb} \Lcal(\vb_1,\pb_1)$. Therefore, for $j \in \clean_k$
\begin{align*}
    &\bp_2^{\top}(\bx_{j,1}-\bx_{j,2}) 
    \\
    &= -(\bx_{j,1}-\bx_{j,2})^{\top}\steps \nabla_{\pb} \Lcal(\vb_t,\pb_t) = (\bx_{j,1}-\bx_{j,2})^{\top} \frac{\steps }{n} \sumn -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot  \bX_i^{\top} \SM' (X_i\pb_t)  \bgamma_i^{t=1} \\
    &=   \frac{\steps }{n} \sumn -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot  \bx_{j,1}^{\top}\bX_i^{\top} \SM' (X_i\pb_t)  \bgamma_i^{t=1} - \frac{\steps }{n} \sumn -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot  \bx_{j,2}^{\top}\bX_i^{\top} \SM' (X_i\pb_t)  \bgamma_i^{t=1} \\
    &=  \frac{\steps }{n} \sum_{i \in [n]} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot (\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1})\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1}(\bx_{j,1}^{\top}\bx_{i,1} + \bx_{j,2}^{\top}\bx_{i,2})   &&\text{ Lemma \ref{lemma: soft-max-derivative}} \\
    &=  \frac{\steps }{n} (-\ell_{1,j}')(\gamma_{j,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{j,2}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{j,1})\alpha_{j,1}(\norm{\bx_{j,1}}^2 + \norm{\bx_{j,2}}^2) \\
    &+   \frac{\steps }{n} \sum_{i \in \clean_k: i \neq j} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot (\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1})\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1}(\bx_{j,1}^{\top}\bx_{i,1}) \\
     &-   \frac{\steps }{n} \sum_{i \in \noise_k: i \neq j} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot (\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1})\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1}(\bx_{j,1}^{\top}\bx_{i,1}) \\
    &+ \frac{\steps }{n} \sum_{i \in [n]:i\neq j} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot (\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1})\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1}(\bx_{j,2}^{\top}\bx_{i,2})~. 
    \end{align*}
Observe that $\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1} = \alpha_{i,2}^{t=1} = 1/2$. In Eqs. \ref{eq: score_clean_t=1} and \ref{eq: score_noise_t=1} we calculate the score differences (e.g. $\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1})$. Overall, we can lower bound the above equation by:
    \begin{align*}
    &\geq \frac{\steps }{4n} \left(m_{\clean} \cdot \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 -2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta - o_n(1))\cdot d(1 - o_n(1))\right) \\
    &+ \frac{\steps }{4n} \left((1-\eta-o_n(1)) \cdot \frac{n}{2} \cdot m_{\clean} \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 -2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta - o_n(1))\frac{d}{n}\signalconst^2 \right) \\
     &- \frac{\steps }{4n} \left((\eta + o_n(1)) \cdot \frac{ n}{2} \cdot M_{\noise} \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta + o_n(1))\frac{d}{n}\signalconst^2 \right) \\
  &- \frac{\steps }{4n} \left(n \cdot M_{\noise} \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta + o_n(1))\sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)} \right).\\
\end{align*}


The first term dominates the last term since $d \gg n\sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}$ (see Assumption \ref{assumption: gd} (item \ref{assumption: gd-high-dimmension})).
The second term dominates the third term for small enough $\eta$ (see Assumption \ref{assumption: gd-label-flipping-rate}). Overall, we obtain that
\begin{align}
    \bp_2^{\top}(\bx_{j,1}-\bx_{j,2}) > 0,
\end{align}
which means that for any $i \in \clean$ we have:
\begin{align} 
\alpha_{i,1}^{t=2} = \frac{1}{1+\exp( -\bp_2^{\top}(\bx_{j,1}-\bx_{j,2}))} > \frac{1}{2}~. \label{eq: p_2-prefer-signal-token-for-clean-sample}
\end{align}

For $j \in \noise_k$,
\begin{align*}
     \bp_2^{\top} (\bx_{j,2}-\bx_{j,1}) &=  \frac{\steps }{n}(-\ell_{1,j}')(\gamma_{j,2}^{t=1}-\gamma_{j,1}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{j,1})\alpha_{j,1}(\norm{\bx_{j,1}}^2 + \norm{\bx_{j,2}}^2) \\
    &-   \frac{\steps }{n} \sum_{i \in \clean_k: i \neq j} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot (\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1})\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1}(\bx_{j,1}^{\top}\bx_{i,1}) \\
    &+   \frac{\steps }{n} \sum_{i \in \noise_k: i \neq j} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot (\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1})\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1}(\bx_{j,1}^{\top}\bx_{i,1}) \\
    &- \frac{\steps }{n} \sum_{i \in [n]:i\neq j} -\ell_{1,i}' \cdot (\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1})(1-\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1})\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1}(\bx_{j,2}^{\top}\bx_{i,2}) &&\text{ Lemma \ref{lemma: soft-max-derivative}} 
    \end{align*}
Observe that $\alpha_{i,1}^{t=1} = \alpha_{i,2}^{t=1} = 1/2$. In Eq.~\ref{eq: score_clean_t=1} and Eq. \ref{eq: score_noise_t=1} we calculate the score differences (e.g. $\gamma_{i,1}^{t=1}-\gamma_{i,2}^{t=1})$. Overall, we can lower bound the above equation by:
    \begin{align*}
    &\geq \frac{\steps }{4n} \left(m_{\noise} \cdot \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta - o_n(1)) \cdot d(1 - o_n(1))\right) \\
    &- \frac{\steps }{4n} \left((1-\eta + o_n(1)) \cdot \frac{n}{2} \cdot M_{\clean} \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 -2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta + o_n(1))\frac{d}{n}\signalconst^2 \right) \\
     &+ \frac{\steps }{4n} \left(|\noise_k|  \cdot m_{\noise}\frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta - o_n(1))\frac{d}{n}\signalconst^2 \right) \\
  &- \frac{\steps }{4n} \left(n \cdot M_{\noise} \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta + o_n(1))\sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)} \right).\\
\end{align*}
Observe that the third term is non-negative. Moreover, we argue that the first term is at least twice the sum of the second and last terms. Indeed, enough to show that
\begin{align*}
&  \left(m_{\noise} \cdot (1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta - o_n(1)) \cdot d(1 - o_n(1))\right) \geq  \\
&2\left( (1 + o_n(1))\cdot\frac{1}{2} \cdot M_{\clean} \cdot  d\signalconst^2 \right) +  2 \left(n \cdot M_{\noise} (1 + 2/\signalconst^2 + o_n(1))\sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)} \right)~,
\end{align*}
which indeed holds since $n\sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)} = d \cdot o_n(1)$, and $ M_{\clean} \cdot \signalconst^2 \leq 0.25$ while $m_{\noise} \geq 0.99$  (see Eqs. \ref{eq: gd_loss_noise_t=1_lower_bound} and \ref{eq: gd_loss_clean_t=1_upper_bound}).
Overall, for any $i \in \noise$ we have that:
\begin{align*}
    \bp_2^{\top} (\bx_{j,2}-\bx_{j,1}) &\geq \frac{\steps }{8n} \left(m_{\noise} \cdot \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta - o_n(1)) \cdot d(1 - o_n(1))\right) \\
    &= \frac{\stepSizeConst }{8 \signalconst^2} \left(m_{\noise} \cdot \frac{\stepSizeConst}{8}(1 + 2/\signalconst^2 -2\eta - o_n(1)) \cdot (1 - o_n(1))\right) \\
    &\geq 2\log(\signalconst),
\end{align*}
where that last inequality holds since $\stepSizeConst \geq 16\signalconst \log(\signalconst)$, which implies that $0.9\stepSizeConst^2 / 64\signalconst^2 \geq 2\log(\signalconst) = \log(\signalconst^2)$.
We conclude that,
\begin{align}
\alpha_{i,2}^{t=2} 
&= \frac{1}{1+\exp( -\bp_2^{\top}(\bx_{j,2}-\bx_{j,1}))} \geq \frac{1}{1+\exp(-\log(\signalconst^2))} = \frac{1}{1 + 1/\signalconst^2} \nonumber
\\
&= \frac{\signalconst^2}{\signalconst^2+1} \geq \frac{\signalconst^2-1}{\signalconst^2} = 1 - 1/\signalconst^2.
\end{align}
We conclude that for any $j \in \noise$ we have that
\begin{align}
    \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} \geq 1 - 1/\signalconst^2, \ \ \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} \leq 1/\signalconst^2. \label{eq: p_2-prefer-noisy-token-for-noisy-sample} 
\end{align}
Together with Eq. \ref{eq: p_2-prefer-signal-token-for-clean-sample}, this proves the third part of the Thm.  \\


\textbf{The classifier $\sign(f(\bX;\vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2}))$ classifies correctly clean training samples.}
Let $(\bX_j = (\bx_{j,1},\bx_{j,2}), y_j)$ for $j \in \clean$. We remind that $\bx_{j,1} = \bmu_k$ for $k \in \{1,2\}$ and $\bx_{2,j} = \bxi_j$. we have that,
    \begin{align*}
       f(\bX_j; \vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2}) = \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} \bv_{2}^{\top}\bx_{j,1} + \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} \bv_{2}^{\top}\bx_{j,2}, 
    \end{align*}
and it suffices to prove that 
\begin{align*}
    y_j(f(\bX_j; \vb_2,\pb_2)) > 0.
\end{align*}
 Indeed,
\begin{align*}
    y_j&f(\bX_j; \vb,\pb) = \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} y_j\bv_2^{\top}\bx_{j,1} + \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} y_j\bv_2^{\top}\bx_{j,2} \\
    &= \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} |\lambda_k|\norm{\bmu_k}^2 + \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} \theta_j^2 \norm{\bxi_j}^2+ \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} y_j \sum_{i\in [n]: i\neq j} y_i\theta_i^{t=2} \bxi_i^{\top}\bxi_{j} &&y_j\lambda_k > 0\\
    &\geq   \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} |\lambda_k|\norm{\bmu_k}^2  - \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} n \max_i |\theta_i| \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}\\
    &\geq   \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} \left(\frac{\beta}{9}\right)\frac{d}{n}\signalconst^2  - \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} n \frac{\beta}{2n}(M_{\noise}+1) \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)} &&\text{Eqs. \ref{eq: gd_bound_theta_noise_t=2}, \ref{eq: gd_lower_bound_lam1_t=2} and \ref{eq: gd_upper_bound_lam2_t=2}}  \\
    &\geq \frac{1}{2} \left(\frac{\beta}{9}\right)\frac{d}{n}\signalconst^2  - \frac{1}{2} n \frac{\beta}{2n}(M_{\noise}+1) \sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)} &&\text{Eq. \ref{eq: p_2-prefer-signal-token-for-clean-sample}}\\
    &> 0, && d \gg n\sqrt{d \log(12n^2/\delta)}
\end{align*}
as required. \\
\textbf{The classifier $\sign(f(\bX;\vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2}))$ classifies correctly noisy training samples.}
Let $(\bX_j = (\bx_{j,1},\bx_{j,2}), y_j)$ for $j \in \noise$. We remind that $\bx_{j,1} = \bmu_k$ for $k \in \{1,2\}$ and $\bx_{2,j} = \bxi_j$. we have that,
    \begin{align*}
       f(\bX_j; \vb_{t=2},\pb_{t=2}) = \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} \bv_{2}^{\top}\bx_{j,1} + \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} \bv_{2}^{\top}\bx_{j,2}, 
    \end{align*}
and it suffices to prove that 
\begin{align*}
    y_j(f(\bX_j; \vb_2,\pb_2)) > 0.
\end{align*}
 Indeed,
\begin{align*}
    y_j&f(\bX_j; \vb,\pb) = \alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} y_j\bv_2^{\top}\bx_{j,1} + \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} y_j\bv_2^{\top}\bx_{j,2} \\
    &= -\alpha_{j,1}^{t=2} |\lambda_k|\norm{\bmu_k}^2 + \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} \theta_j^2 \norm{\bxi_j}^2+ \alpha_{j,2}^{t=2} y_j \sum_{i\in [n]: i\neq j} y_i\theta_i^{t=2} \bxi_i^{\top}\bxi_{j} &&y_j\lambda_k < 0\\
